 SELECT callsign, final_score, name, exchange FROM contest_results WHERE year = 2026 AND is_valid = 1 AND location_type in ('LA-FIXED', 'LA-ROVER') ORDER BY final_score DESC; 


SELECT callsign, category_rank FROM contest_results
WHERE cat = 'TSL'  ORDER BY rank DESC;

WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT id, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY final_score DESC) AS calculated_rank
    FROM contest_results
    WHERE year = 2026 AND cat = 'TSL'
)
UPDATE contest_results
SET category_rank = ranked.calculated_rank
FROM ranked
WHERE contest_results.id = ranked.id;

WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT year, callsign, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY final_score DESC) AS calculated_rank
    FROM contest_results
    WHERE year = 2026 AND cat = 'TSL'
)
UPDATE contest_results
SET category_rank = ranked.calculated_rank
FROM ranked
WHERE contest_results.year = ranked.year
  AND contest_results.callsign = ranked.callsign;

        result['dxcc_code'] = int(callsign_info['adif'])
        result['dxcc_entity'] = self.dxcc_entities[result['dxcc_code']]

CROSS_CHECKING
A QSO looks like this:
QSO: 14252 PH 2023-04-01 1514 AA0FO         59  KS      K5LSU         59  EBR
the CHECK QSO would look like this:
QSO: 14252 PH 2023-04-01 1517 K5LSU         59  EBR     AA0FO         59  KS

General approach. Process all QSO's as if they cross-checked and write their results into the DB. A match for this QSO is looked for in the "xcheck" table. If there was no match in the table, or the two QSOs fail to match, record that fact. If they do match, then don't record anything for the sending op and remove any record of this QSO for the receiving op in the table.

Once all logs are processed, we need to go through the xcheck and for each callsign in there, we need to adjust their stats in the DB. We reduce qso points and potentially mults

The two contacts must match EXACTLY on the two callsigns and the frequency. So when encountering the first QSO, we need to look for a QSO in the cross-check Dict table of seen QSOs that has this key: remote operator's callsign. The value for this key is a list of Dict's. Each element in te list represents a QSO of his that was processed without a corresponding cross-check QSO. The keys in each Dict are receiving callsign, time (not date), and whether this QSO was provisionally couned as a MULT in addition to its QSO points.

If that record is in the table, we check that the times are within 10 minutes (???) of each other. 
IF they are, then the QSO currently beoing processed "CHECKS" and can count for points. The record for the QSO of the receiving operator is removed. If that was the last un-matched QSO for that callsign, the entire record for that callsign is removed.

If they do not check, then we do a few things:
(1) count this QSO for the current user for points and mults and continue processing. 
(2) write this unmatched QSO for the current op in the table.
(2) the remote opoerator (EBR) needs to be reprocessed because he was given provisional 

If that record is NOT in the table, then this QSO is PROVISIONALLY counted for points

that's workijng great! Next step is to save the individual rankings for each operator in the database. We created a field in the content_results table called rankings that is a dict. The idea I had was to add each applicable ranking into this dict, with the key being a code for the "category" and the value being his numerical rank. I want you to think about whether this is a good way to go, or whether we should make another table that has the rankings for each operator, indexed the existing table by year+callsign. We might end up with literally dozens of ranking categories, but each individual user will probably only be ranked in less than a dozen. I have created a constant dict - RANKINGS - that has as its key  the short code for the category, and the value is the descriptive string for that category to be used in displays. For example:
'LRS': 'Louisiana - Rover SSB (phone) Mode',
 'LRM': 'Louisiana - Rover MIXED Modes (SSB, CW, Digital',
 'NQ': 'Non Louisiana - QRP Power',
 'NL': ‘Non Louisiana - LOW Power' …
I have also changed the LEADERBOARD list. The dicts that have a ‘title’ key used to have a string to display as the title of the grid, but I have changed it so that the value for the ‘title’ key is now the key from the RANKINGS dict. So where we used to have something like ‘title’: ‘Non Louisiana - QRP Power', you now have ‘title’: ‘NQ’. You need to use this key to get the title string from the RANKINGS dict. If you are interested, please email laqp.w5gad.org. You can only operate the strict schedule we end up making to avoid multiple operators using the sme band&mode at the same time.

I have not yet integrated the Docker code you created, nor the individual user lookup. I want to work on storing all the individual info for the user in the database and creating the leaderboards. This is how I want it to work. First the sqlite3 log records need to be keyed on Year and Callsign. An operator may participate in the contest in different years and have multiple records in the database, one for each year. When a log file for a year and callsign is processed, it should OVERWRITE a record in the DB for that same year and callsign. In other words it is a replacement. The revised object that is used in scoring an individual log is below. Note that I have added the year. When we are finished scoring the user and have displayed his results in the upload form, we should write this object to the database, keyed by year and callsign. We do NOT need to include the _qso_lines nor the _headers. We do, however, need to add another field, called “rankings”,  that will be used for the rankings when all the logs are received. This is a python dict that has been jasonified to store in the db. It is of the form, {‘rank1’: int, ‘rank2’: int. . .} where the ‘rank1’ is the category that the user is being ranked in, and the integer is his place in that category among all the other operators who represent that category. So for example, if he used only CW, and he was 45th out of 125 operators who were in that category, then his rank dict might look like this: {‘cw’: 45} and when we display his ranking,   we display it at CW: 45/100. We will do this procedure in parts. First please modify the web.py code and the batch.py code (both uploaded in current form) to write the db record described above for each log successfully processed. The “rankings” field will of course be empty but present in the record.


Here is the proposed method to do this, but if you have a better approach tell me and we can discuss it. There is a deadline for all the operators to have their logs uploaded. We will do the leaderboards on that date, using a batch program, called results.py. This program will operate as follows. For each category of ranking (which are listed below), the code will (a) get a list of all the logs in that category sorted by the operator’s total score. (b) remember the count of this list, (c) iterate through all the db records and update the “results” dict in the db record to reflect the operator’s rank in this category. Note that a single operator may be in multiple ranking categories, e.g. CW and POTA, and HIGH power, and will have a different ranking in each. The results.py batch app will do more, but let’s start with creating the database for each user

The next step is to create a batch app called results.py  It goes through a list of result categories - or leaderboards, that is in config.py called LEADERBOARDS. does two things. ItHere is the proposed method to do this, but if you have a better approach tell me and we can discuss it. There is a deadline for all the operators to have their logs uploaded. We will do the leaderboards on that date, using a batch program, called results.py. This program will operate as follows. For each category of ranking (which are listed below), the code will (a) get a list of all the logs in that category sorted by the operator’s total score. (b) remember the count of this list, (c) iterate through all the db records and update the “results” dict in the db record to reflect the operator’s rank in this category. Note that a single operator may be in multiple ranking categories, e.g. CW and POTA, and HIGH power, and will have a different ranking in each.

Pamela,

It could be that we are just caught up in the technical jargon of money ownership. I am not a tax or finance person nor an attorney, but here is some of what I have ready. I imagine most of your clients have access to tax and legal advice, which we don’t, so I am hoping the finance and legal folks at GNOF know what I am talking about here.

It appears that to get the liability protection we want, we must technically give the GNOF "variance power" (ultimate legal control). In practice, foundations like GNOF will usually follow the client’s advice to release funds for our mission (repairing towers, etc.), but they legally must have the final say to validate the transfer of assets.

It also seems that this approach is not 100% effective in shielding assets. Even though we would not legally own and control the funds, accounting rules (FASB ASC 958) often require us to still list them as an asset ("Beneficial Interest in Assets Held by Others") on our own financial statements. This means a savvy plaintiff's lawyer might see them on your balance sheet and try to argue they are available assets, even if legally they aren't.
​

73
Brownell  KJ5BYZ
(504) 521-6744
